Retrocells
Robert Paré

Abstract
The notion of retrocell in a double category with companions is introduced and its basic properties established. Explicit descriptions in some of the usual double categories are given. Monads in a double category provide an important example where retrocells arise naturally. Cofunctors appear as a special case. The motivating example of vertically closed double categories is treated in some detail.



\dedication

For Marta Bunge, constant friend for over half a century

Introduction

In
[
17
]
an in-depth study of the double category
{\mathbb{R}}{\rm ing}
of
rings, homomorphisms, bimodules and linear maps was made, and several
interesting features were uncovered. It became apparent that considering this
double category, rather than the category of rings and homomorphisms or the
bicategory of bimodules, could provide some important insights into the nature
of rings and modules.

An important property of the bicategory of bimodules is that it is biclosed,
i.e. the
\otimes
has right adjoints in each variable so that we have bijections
of linear maps

M\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>N\obslash_{T}P

N\otimes_{S}M\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>P

N\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>P\oslash_{R}M

for bimodules

\textstyle{R}
\textstyle{T\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}
\scriptstyle{P}
\textstyle{S}
\textstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{M}
\textstyle{S}
\textstyle{T\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}
\scriptstyle{N}

We use (a slight modification of Lambek’s notation for the hom bimodules

[
14
]
):
P\oslash_{R}M
is the
T\mbox{-}S
bimodule of

R
-linear maps
M\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>P
, and
T
-linear for
N\obslash_{T}P
.
Both
P\oslash_{R}M
and
N\obslash_{T}P
are covariant in
P

but contravariant in the other variables. This is for
2
-cells in the
bicategory
{\cal{B}}{\it im}
but it does not extend to cells in the double
category
{\mathbb{R}}{\rm ing}
, which casts a shadow on our
contention that
{\mathbb{R}}{\rm ing}
works better than
{\cal{B}}{\it im}
.

The way out of this dilemma is hinted at in the commuter cells of
[
11
]

(there called commutative cells) introduced to deal with the universal
property of internal comma objects. That is, to use companions to define
new cells, which we call retrocells below, and thus recover functoriality.

After a quick review of companions in Section 1, we introduce retrocells
in Section 2 and see that they are the cells of a new double category,
and if we apply this construction twice, we get the original double category, up to
isomorphism.

Section 3 extends the mates calculus to double categories where
we see retrocells appearing as the mates of standard cells. A careful
study of dualities in Section 4 completes this.

Retrocells in the standard double categories whose vertical arrows
are spans, relations, profunctors or
{\bf V}
-matrices are
analyzed in Section 5. They correspond to various sorts of liftings
reminiscent of fibrations.

Section 6 studies retrocells in the context of monads in a double
category. It is seen that, while Kleisli objects are certain kinds of
universal cells, Eilenberg-Moore objects are universal retrocells.
In
{\mathbb{S}}{\rm pan}{\bf A}
, monads are
category objects in
{\bf A}
and internal functors are cells
preserving identities and multiplication. Retrocells, on the other hand,
give cofunctors.

In Section 7 we extend Shulman’s closed equipments to general
double categories, and establish the functoriality of internal homs,
covariant in one variable and retrovariant in the other, formulated
in terms of “twisted cospans”.

We end in Section 8 by re-examining commuter cells in the light
of retrocells and see that this leads to an interesting triple category,
though we do not pursue the triple category aspect.

The results of this paper were presented in preliminary form at CT2019
in Edinburgh and in the MIT Categories Seminar in October 2020. We
thank Bryce Clarke, Matt Di Meglio and David Spivak for expressing their
interest in retrocells. We also thank the anonymous referee for a careful
reading and numerous suggestions resulting in a much better presentation.

1
Companions

The whole paper will be concerned with double categories that have
companions, so we recall the definition, principal properties we will use,
and establish some notation (see
[
10
]
for more details).

2
Retrocells

has a companion,

The well-known mates calculus says that if we have functors

Note that the statement of the corollary does not refer to
retrocells or companions but it does not seem possible to
prove it directly without companions. The infamous pinwheel

[
5
]
pops up in all attempts to do so.

4
Coretrocells

There is a dual situation giving two more bijections in the presence
of right adjoints, but the notion of retrocell is not self-dual. In fact
there is a dual notion, coretrocell, which also comes up in practice
as we will see later.

Like for
2
-categories there are duals op and co for double
categories.
{\mathbb{A}}^{op}

has the horizontal direction reversed and
{\mathbb{A}}^{co}
the
vertical. If
{\mathbb{A}}
has companions there is no reason why

{\mathbb{A}}^{op}
or
{\mathbb{A}}^{co}
should, and even if they
did there is no relation between the retrocells there and those of

{\mathbb{A}}
. Companions in
{\mathbb{A}}^{op}
or

{\mathbb{A}}^{co}
correspond to conjoints in
{\mathbb{A}}

and we will use these to define coretrocells.

For completeness we recall the notion of conjoint. More details
can be found in
[
9
]
.

Definition 0
.

Let
f\colon A\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>B
be a horizontal arrow in
{\mathbb{A}}
.
A conjoint for
f
is a vertical arrow

As we said, this is the vertical dual of the notion of companion
and therefore has the corresponding properties. They are unique

is
usually

, which we will assume.

Definition 0
.

Suppose that in
{\mathbb{A}}
every horizontal arrow
f
has
a conjoint
f^{*}
, then a coretrocell

\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{v}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{B}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{w}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}

is a (standard) cell

\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{g^{*}}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{C}

\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{A}

in
{\mathbb{A}}
.

Coretrocells are retrocells in
{\mathbb{A}}^{co}
. So all
properties of retrocells dualize to coretrocells. In particular
we have a double category
{\mathbb{A}}^{cor}
whose cells
are coretrocells. Dualities can be confusing so we list them
here.

Proposition 0
.

(1) If
{\mathbb{A}}
has conjoints then
{\mathbb{A}}^{op}
and

{\mathbb{A}}^{co}
have companions and

(a)
({\mathbb{A}}^{cor})^{op}=({\mathbb{A}}^{op})^{ret}

(b)
({\mathbb{A}}^{cor})^{co}=({\mathbb{A}}^{co})^{ret}

(2) If
{\mathbb{A}}
has companions then
{\mathbb{A}}^{op}
and

{\mathbb{A}}^{co}
have conjoints and

(a)
({\mathbb{A}}^{ret})^{op}=({\mathbb{A}}^{op})^{cor}

(b)
({\mathbb{A}}^{ret})^{co}=({\mathbb{A}}^{co})^{cor}

(3) Under the above conditions

(a)
({\mathbb{A}}^{ret})^{coop}=({\mathbb{A}}^{coop})^{ret}

(b)
({\mathbb{A}}^{cor})^{coop}=({\mathbb{A}}^{coop})^{cor}
.

Passing between
{\mathbb{A}}
and
{\mathbb{A}}^{co}
switches
left adjoints to right (both horizontal and vertical), switches companions
and conjoints, and retrocells with coretrocells. Thus we get the dual
theorem for mates.

Theorem 4.13
.

Assume
{\mathbb{A}}
has conjoints.

(1) If
x
is right adjoint to
v
and
y
right adjoint to
w
,
then there is a bijection between cells
\alpha
and coretrocells

\beta

\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{v}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{B}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{w}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\alpha}
\textstyle{\longleftrightarrow}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{B\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{A}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{B\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}

\scriptstyle{\beta}

(2) If

and

,
then there is a bijection between cells
\alpha
and coretrocells

\gamma

Whereas we think of companions as vertical arrows isomorphic
to horizontal ones, it makes sense to think of a cell
\alpha

as above as a cell

\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{g_{*}}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{D}

\textstyle{\scriptstyle\widehat{\alpha}}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{B}

\textstyle{C}

\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{A}

(which it corresponds to bijectively) and reversing its direction would
give a natural notion of a cell in the opposite direction, thus giving
retrocells. Coretrocells, on the other hand, are less intuitive. We
think of conjoints as vertical arrows adjoint to horizontal ones, and
although there is a bijection between cells
\alpha
and cells

\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{C\hbox to0.0pt{\ ,\hss}}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{C}

\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{A}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{w}

this is more in the nature of a proposition than a tautology.
Nevertheless, formally the two bijections are dual, so have
the same status. Reversing the direction of the
\alpha^{\vee}

gives us coretrocells, and they do come up in practice as we will
see in the next sections.

This example shows well the difference between retrocells
and coretrocells and their comparison with actual cells.

The story for relations is much the same. If
{\bf A}
is a
regular category and

\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{B}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{S}

is a boundary in
{\mathbb{R}}{\rm el}{\bf A}
, i.e.
f
and
g

are morphisms and
R
and
S
are relations, then in the
internal language of
A
, there is a (necessarily unique) cell
iff

a\sim_{R}c\Rightarrow fa\sim_{S}gc\ ,

there is a retrocell iff

fa\sim_{S}d\Rightarrow\exists c(a\sim_{R}c\wedge gc=d)

and a coretrocell iff

b\sim_{S}gc\quad\Rightarrow\quad\exists a(a\sim_{R}c\wedge fa=b).

Profunctors are the relations of the
{\cal C}{\it at}
world. There is a double
category which we call
{\mathbb{C}}{\rm at}
whose objects are small categories,
horizontal arrows functors, vertical arrows profunctors, and cells
the appropriate natural transformations. In a typical cell

\textstyle{D}

\textstyle{\scriptstyle{[}\alpha_{ac}{]}}

\alpha_{ac}\colon V_{ac}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>W_{fa,gc}.

Vertical composition is matrix multiplication

[X_{ce}]\otimes[V_{ac}]=[\sum_{c\in C}X_{ce}\otimes V_{ac}].

Every horizontal arrow has a companion

f_{*}=[\Delta_{fa,b}]

and a conjoint

f^{*}=[\Delta_{b,fa}]

where
\Delta
is the “Kronecker delta”

\Delta_{b,b^{\prime}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}I&\mbox{if}&b=b^{\prime}\\
0&\mbox{if}&b\neq b^{\prime}.\end{array}\right.

A retrocell

\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{{[}V_{ac}{]}}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{B}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{{[}W_{bd}{]}}
\scriptstyle{\phi}

is an
A\times D
matrix
[\phi_{ad}]

\phi_{ad}\colon W_{fa,d}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\sum_{gc=d}V_{ac}\ .

For example, if

, and we again represent

with
gc_{i}=d

\textstyle{fa}
\textstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{w}
\textstyle{a}
\textstyle{fa}
\textstyle{a}
\textstyle{c_{i}}
\scriptstyle{v_{i}}
\textstyle{c_{i}}
\textstyle{d}
\textstyle{(i=1,...,n)\ .}

Of course the dual situation holds for coretrocells
\psi
.

So we see that (co)retrocells in each case give liftings but
of a type adapted to the situation. For spans they are
uniquely specified, for relations they exist but are not
specified, for profunctors only up to a connectedness
condition and for matrices of Abelian groups we get a finite
number of them.

6
Monads

A monad in
{\cal C}{\it at}
is a quadruple
({\bf A},T,\eta,\mu)
where

{\bf A}
is a category,
T\colon{\bf A}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\bf A}
an endofunctor,

\eta\colon 1_{\bf A}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>T
and
\mu\colon T^{2}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>T
natural
transformations satisfying the well-known unit and associativity laws.
In
[
19
]
Street introduced morphisms of monads

(F,\phi)\colon({\bf A},T,\eta,\mu)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>({\bf B},S,\kappa,\nu)

as functors
F\colon{\bf A}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\bf B}
together with a natural
transformation

\textstyle{\bf A}
\textstyle{\bf B}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\bf A}
\textstyle{\bf A}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{\bf A}
\textstyle{\bf B}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\bf B}
\textstyle{\bf B}

respecting units and multiplications in the obvious way. He
called these monad functors, now called lax monad morphisms

[
15

). This was done, not just in

what he called monad opfunctors, i.e. oplax morphisms of
monads, with the
\phi
in the opposite direction.

The lax morphisms work well with Eilenberg-Moore algebras,
giving a functor

0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>SFB).

The story for monads in a double category is this (see
[
8
,
7
]
, though
note that there horizontal and vertical are reversed). In general
we just get one kind of morphism, the oplax ones. If we have
companions then we also get the lax ones, and if we also have
conjoints we have another kind. The
2
-category case
considered by Street corresponds to the double category of
coquintets which has companions but not conjoints.

Let
{\mathbb{A}}
be a double category. A vertical

These are the oplax morphisms referred to above.

There are also vertical morphisms of monads, “bimodules”, whose
composition requires certain well-behaved coequalizers. They are
interesting (see e.g.
[
18
]
), of course, but will not concern us here.

If
{\mathbb{A}}
has companions we can also define
retromorphisms of monads. (See
[
3
,
16
]
.)

Proposition 0
.

The identity retrocell is a retromorphism
(A,t,\eta,\mu)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>(A,t,\eta,\mu)
. The composite of two retromorphisms of
monads is again one.

Proof.

Easy calculation.

∎

For a monad
t=(A,t,\eta,\mu)
, Kleisli is a colimit construction,
a universal morphism of the form

(A,t,\eta,\mu)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>(X,\id_{X},1,1).

Just by universality, if we have a morphism of monads
(h,\psi)\colon(A,t,\eta,\mu)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>(B,s,\kappa,\nu)

This does not work for Eilenberg-Moore objects. Asking for a universal
morphism of the form

(X,\id_{X},1,1)\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>(A,t,\eta,\mu)

is not the right thing as can be seen from the usual

but also in general. For such a morphism

\scriptstyle{t}

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{A}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{X}

\textstyle{A}

\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{A}

\textstyle{A}

\textstyle{A}

Example 0
.

Let
\cal{A}
be a
2
-category and
{\mathbb{Q}}{\cal{A}}
the
double category of quintets in
\cal{A}
. Recall that a cell in
{\mathbb{Q}}{\cal{A}}

is a quintet in
\cal{A}

\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{D\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{B}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{D\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}}
\scriptstyle{k}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}

Every horizontal arrow
f
has a companion, namely
f
itself
but viewed as a vertical arrow. A (vertical) monad in
{\mathbb{Q}}{\cal{A}}

is a comonad in
\cal{A}
. A morphism of monads in
{\mathbb{Q}}{\cal{A}}

is then a lax morphism of comonads, and a retromorphism of monads in

{\mathbb{Q}}{\cal{A}}
is an oplax morphism of comonads in
\cal{A}
.

To make the connection with Street’s monad functors and opfunctors,
we must take coquintets (the
\alpha
in the opposite direction)

{\rm co}{\mathbb{Q}}{\cal{A}}
. Now a monad in
{\rm co}{\mathbb{Q}}{\cal{A}}

is a monad in
\cal{A}
, a monad morphism in
{\rm co}{\mathbb{Q}}{\cal{A}}

is an oplax morphism of monads, i.e. a monad opfunctor in
\cal{A}
,
whereas a retromorphism of monads is now a lax morphism of monads,
i.e. a monad functor.

It is unfortunate that the most natural morphisms from a double category
point of view are not the established ones in the literature. At the time of

[
19
]
, people were more interested in the Eilenberg-Moore algebras
for a monad as a generalization of Lawvere theories and their algebras,
so it was natural to choose the monad morphisms that worked well
with those, namely lax morphisms, as monad functors. Now, with the
advent of categorical computer science, Kleisli categories have come
into their own, and it is not so clear what the leading concept is, and
double category theory suggests that it may well be the oplax
morphisms.

.

{\bf C}
be a category with (a choice of) pullbacks. As is

is a
category object in
{\bf C}
. A morphism of monads in

{\mathbb{S}}{\rm pan}{\bf C}
is an internal functor.

This again illustrates well the difference between retromorphisms
and coretromorphisms and, at the same time, the symmetry of
the concepts. They all move objects forward. Functors move
arrows forward

7
Closed double categories

Many bicategories that come up in practice are closed, i.e.
composition
\otimes
has right adjoints in each variable,

Q\otimes(-)\dashv Q\obslash(\ )

(\ )\otimes P\dashv(\ )\oslash P\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}

Example 0
.

If
{\bf A}

).

\prod_{A\times\tau_{0}}
. So, for
A\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.47516pt\raise 5.00694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sigma_{0}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 8.74355pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>S\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.47516pt\raise 5.00694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sigma_{1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 8.74355pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 23.48709pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>C
,

T\obslash_{C}S=\prod_{A\times\tau_{0}}(A\times\tau_{1})^{*}S\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}

If we interpret this for
{\bf A}={\bf Set}
, in terms of fibers

(T\obslash_{C}S)_{ab}=\prod_{c}S_{ac}^{T_{bc}}\ .

The situation for
\oslash_{A}
is similar

(S\oslash_{A}R)_{bc}=\prod_{a}S_{ac}^{R_{ab}}\ .

These bicategories, and in fact most bicategories that occur in
practice, are the vertical bicategories of naturally occurring double
categories. So a definition of a (vertically) closed double category
would seem in order. And indeed Shulman in
[
18
]
did
give one. A double category is closed if its vertical bicategory is.
This definition was taken up by Koudenburg
[
13
]
in
his work on pointwise Kan extensions. But both were working
with “equipments”, double categories with companions and
conjoints. Something more is needed for general double
categories.

{\mathbb{A}}

Theorem 7.29
.

For
{\mathbb{A}}

\mathchoice{\mathop{\backslash\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{\bullet}$}}}{\mathop{\backslash\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{\bullet}$}}}{\mathop{\scriptstyle{\backslash\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}$}}}}{\mathop{\scriptstyle{\backslash\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}$}}}}\ \colon{\bf TC}({\mathbb{A}})\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{\bf A}_{1}\hbox to0.0pt{\ .\hss}

For identities

∎

Right closure is dual but the duality is op, switching the direction of
vertical arrows which switches companions with conjoints and retrocells
with coretrocells. We outline the changes.

Definition 0
.

(Shulman)
{\mathbb{A}}
has globular right homs if
for every
x
,

.

A double category
{\mathbb{A}}
is closed if it is right
closed and left closed.

∎

We end by acknowledging the “triple category in the room”. The
cubes we have been discussing are clearly the triple cells of a triple
category
{\mathfrak{Ret}}{\mathbb{A}}
. We orient the cubes to
be in line with our intercategories conventions of
[
12
]

where the faces of the cubes are horizontal, vertical (left and right),
and basic (front and back) in decreasing order of strictness (or
fancyness). The order here will be commutative, cell, and retrocell.

1.

Objects are the objects of
{\mathbb{A}}
, (
A,A^{\prime},B,..
)

2.

Transversal arrows are the horizontal arrows of
{\mathbb{A}}
,
(
f,f^{\prime},g,g^{\prime}
)

3.

Horizontal arrows are the horizontal arrows of
{\mathbb{A}}
,
(
a,b,c,d
)

4.

Vertical arrows are the vertical arrows of
{\mathbb{A}}
,
(
v,v^{\prime},w,w^{\prime}
)

5.

Horizontal cells are commutative squares of horizontal arrows

6.

Vertical cells are double cells in
{\mathbb{A}}
, (
